ParlaMint-EE_2022-01-26_U683	Honourable Riigikogu! Ladies and gentlemen, Good listeners! Last spring, in the country's long-term strategy "Estonia 2035" adopted here in the Riigikogu, we set the goal of becoming an innovative country that values the creation and use of knowledge. Estonia's goal is to become a knowledge society where we can always rely on knowledge in our choices and can use this knowledge to promote the well-being and economy of Estonian people. Without R &amp; D, we will not really solve any development needs, whether they belong to the environment, health, entrepreneurship or public administration. We have reached a stage in the development of society where the importance of R &amp; D-enabled solutions is growing in all sectors and sectors. The problems we are facing today in society and in the wider world have become much more complex and multifaceted than they were 10 or 20 years ago. All of this is to be expected, which also means repeating increased responsibility for decision-making that affects the state and society. Good Riigikogu! The financing of R &amp; D is guaranteed by the Coalition Agreement and is growing. Science is once again balanced on a competitive and basic basis over many years. We have increased both funding by another EUR 6 million in the year that began. Instead of investing, universities and companies have increased the cost of researchers' salaries, because only people can create new knowledge, not houses or complex technologies. In the last year, labour costs in R &amp; D increased by 14%. The government has complied with the broadly agreed objective of research funding. In the final year, 1% of gross domestic product was allocated from the state budget to support R &amp; D. That is also the case this year. Despite extraordinary times in the energy, health, security and climate crisis, we have raised public funding for research and development. Substantial changes are taking place in private R &amp; D. In 2019 the R &amp; D expenditure of the Estonian private sector passed public expenditure. This is a long-awaited development. They increased further in 2020, exceeding 1% of gross domestic product (GDP) for the first time in 10 years. A total of EUR 481 million was spent on R &amp; D in the public and private sectors, which is 115 million more than two years earlier. And the fruits of this change we will see soon. In terms of R &amp; D expenditure as a ratio to gross domestic product, we were 11th in the second consecutive year in comparison with the countries of the European Union. There is still a lot of work to be done to catch up with the Nordic countries, but with 1.8% of our R &amp; D expenditure, we have done 0.7% of Latvia, 1.2% of Lithuania and 1.4% of Poland better. The R &amp; D expenditure of the Estonian private sector exceeded the absolute values of the Latvian private sector more than four times. Companies in Lithuania bigger than us spent the same amount on development with us. Estonian employers have taken their signature very seriously on the science agreement. The desire to acknowledge the importance of R &amp; D investment through success stories has been launched by a two-percent club. These are companies that invest more than 2% of their turnover in R &amp; D or at least EUR million per year. In order to boost innovation in other businesses, an innovation launch shop has been set up within the Employers' Confederation. The number of researchers and engineers in the business sector is increasing and is also getting younger. I believe that this trend will also support the continued development of Estonian industrial enterprises and the achievement of the productivity target set for 2035. In addition to strong basic research, the volume of applied research and experimental and development work is also growing strongly. This means that we are increasingly able to make the knowledge we have created work for people's well-being and the economy. A growing part of the state budget's R &amp; D expenditure reaches researchers through ministries, helping the state to make smarter choices in addressing the development needs of society. In 2020, the volume of applied research in the public sector increased by half and in universities by a whole quarter. Never before has the state commissioned so much science to solve the problems of governance. In some areas of government, we also set an example for our neighbours. The participation of Estonian researchers in NATO's research organisation is the largest ratio to defence costs in all countries. Studies related to the coronal apandemia and their impact on society are also good examples: Professor Krista Fischer's models, Professor Ruth Kalda's monitoring data, Professor Tanel Tenson's waste water samples. Based on these analyses, the government has made decisions that directly affect society in order to combat the spread of the Coronavirus. This cooperation experience has been good in many ways and instructive and constructive for politicians, researchers as well as officials and society at large. A new applied research programme has successfully started to support businesses in launching, financing and finding suitable partners. During the first year of operation, more than 200 Estonian companies turned to EAS to advise on development plans, and nearly 100 technology projects have been evaluated. Two calls for proposals have resulted in the launch of EUR 43 million worth of research and development, which will change the technologies, processes and products used by companies to date. Businesses' interest in applied research has been so high that we have already increased the volume of the programme. We have planned 20 million for these developments in 2022. As an example, Cleveron is developing a new generation of parcel robots with the help of its programme, Cleveron is developing a new generation of delivery robots that will help deliver the goods ordered from the e-shop to the consumer comfortably. In cooperation with scientists at Tallinn University of Technology, the company develops RS-OSA technologies to use oil shale ash as a raw material. The focus of the development work of Eesti Energia and Viru Chemistry Group is to recycle plastic waste that we cannot recycle today. The contribution of researchers to a more knowledge-based economy and society has not been greater. Universities have also launched various units and activities to support the transfer of knowledge and technology. In the end of the year, Antegenes, a branch of personal medicine based on researchers at the University of Tartu, managed to reach a large investment and strategic partnership with a foreign company. This brings innovative genetic tests developed by Estonian scientists for cancer prevention to the UK market. In the light of the energy crisis, of course, the research-based collaboration of researchers at Tallinn University of Technology with KredEx and construction companies will be remembered, which will develop and pilot the industrial insulation of apartment buildings. This will help us both to meet our climate goals by reducing energy consumption, also reducing the financial pressure on families to cope. The energy use of our commercial buildings is also anything but sustainable. The need for knowledge transfer is high and growing in this respect. Happily, we are getting more and more of these examples every year. Good Riigikogu! There is, of course, much work to be done. The development needs of Estonian society are an opportunity for economic and social innovation. Estonia's goal in implementing the green and digital revolution must be to increase the development and well-being of society. American astronaut Jim Lovell has said that human reaching the moon was not a miracle, it was a decision. In order to succeed in big things, we need many people, institutions and companies who make this decision for themselves. Skeleton Technologies, together with Tallinn University of Technology, has set out to turn Estonia into a centre for research and development of high-tech energy storage systems. This is an excellent example of cooperation between companies, researchers and also the state, a new ecosystem in a technology-intensive field that benefits the Estonian economy, the environment and science, but also the wider development of green technologies. Professor Enn Lust has been led by researchers from the University of Tartu by H2Electro to build an electrifier based on novel technology. This is an important link in the green hydrogen production cycle, which helps to store wind and solar energy as hydrogen. In cooperation with Auve Tech, researchers from the same institute completed the first fuel cell created in Estonia that can convert hydrogen into electricity. We must also contribute to the emergence of such ecosystems in other areas, be they personal medicine, smart food production or artificial intelligence, to name but a few. The government is also creating new equity instruments to contribute to the development of research-intensive companies in Estonia. Investment in innovative technologies involves risks, but success increases our smart exports and helps meet our climate goals. A large part of the actions of the Fair Transition Fund and the Refinancing Facility are also aimed at supporting innovation and smart growth. However, there is a great deal of work ahead so that their impact on the Estonian economy and regional development can be unleashed. Science is free and we are proud of the autonomy of our research institutions in Estonia for a reason. However, we also need the contribution of basic sciences to address the country's development needs. In the long term, however, innovation is most likely to come from basic sciences, although it may take some time to value the results there. Unlike applied research and development grants, we will not be writing research topics in the future in support of basic research. However, all scientists can make an important contribution to these changes through their choices. In order to reach the targets set, we need more staff with scientific experience. In terms of the number of researchers per 1000 employees, we are falling behind many OECD countries. Doctoral degrees are only protected as a ratio of 87% of the European Union average. We do not want to compare ourselves here with Latvia, where it is only 22%, or Lithuania, where it is 48%, because our example is Finland and Sweden, where both are protected by 139% compared to the European average of doctoral degrees. The number of doctoral degrees defended in our universities must be turned to an upward trend. I agree with President Alar Karis that the standard of wisdom and education is not the number of diplomas or degrees, but the ability to use your knowledge. But a scientist is raised in doctoral studies. We need to significantly increase young people's interest in doctoral studies and careers in order to ensure the sustainability of higher education and science in Estonian. We do not have too many foreign doctoral students; we have too few Estonian doctoral students. The university can only hire faculty members from among people with a doctorate. Only people who speak Estonian with a doctorate can offer higher education in Estonian. Businesses and public authorities are also increasingly in need of their assistance. As a solution, the government has sent a doctoral reform package to the Riigikogu for discussion. Its aim is to make doctoral studies as career choices more attractive, better remunerated and to create different paths to go through it. In order to implement the reform, EUR 6 million has been allocated to the state budget for 2022. Now universities, along with their partners, have to fill it in. The plan is to change the preparation of doctoral students in such a way that an increasing number of them take up work and take Estonia's life outside universities. This brings us closer to the goal of a knowledge-intensive state and society. The Estonian development plan for R &amp; D, innovation and entrepreneurship was completed in the end of the year, Estonian development plan for R &amp; D, innovation and entrepreneurship was completed until 2035 or TAIE. The expectation of the new strategy is that it will further increase the joint contribution of the research community and businesses to the development of society and the economy. We have also strengthened the role of the Research and Development Council, or TAN, in strategic decisions on R &amp; D and innovation. In TAN, innovation and knowledge transfer are the strongest competence ever. The work is beginning with the Enterprise Innovation Foundation established on the basis of EAS and KredEx. The Riigikogu will soon have meaningful discussions with a new law on R &amp; D and innovation management. In order to achieve the objectives set out in the R&D&I Development Plan, we also need to review the roles of governance and organisation, financing and various actors in the field. For the first time in the framework of the same law, we are regulating the national organisation of R &amp; D as well as innovation, towards more comprehensive policy making. When preparing the new law, we have not forgotten our values, roots, and language. No one does our national science unless we do it ourselves. This is why we propose in the new law that the model of scientific performance finance should include indicators related to the development of national sciences alongside the general quality and performance indicators, the indicators for business cooperation and contributing to the development of society. This is important to us because the promotion of national sciences is a prerequisite for the survival of Estonian culture and state. Estonia is a small country that is in some ways good. At the same time, we will inevitably have fewer people than the big ones, the means to deal with everything equally well and in depth. We have to make choices. At the end of the year, we pushed ahead with the work of decorating the five R &amp; D focus areas selected in the development plan. These five areas include digital solutions, health technologies, resource enhancement, sustainable energy solutions and viable Estonia, which focuses on areas related to the development of Estonian society, nationality and cultural space. We will continue to negotiate with research institutions and businesses, professional associations and ministries to specify the activities and funding opportunities needed for the development of selected areas. These areas are very diverse, so their needs are also very diverse. We want to reach all the roadmaps for the development of focus areas during this spring to launch these necessary measures already in the autumn. Good listeners! ParlaMint-EE_2022-01-26_U744	Let's move on to our agenda. The eighth item is the draft law amending the International Sanctions Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and the Flag Law of the ship and the Ship Registers Act, bill number 492 SE, first reading. And I call here the Foreign Minister Eva-Maria Liimetsa as a waitress for the Riigikogu's speech chair. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2022-01-26_U745	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Honourable members of the Riigikogu! The draft is primarily designed to ensure that we implement financial sanctions in accordance with international standards. Through better financial sanctions, we will ensure a better economic environment in the financial sector. It is also important to preserve Estonia's reputation and enhances Estonia's credibility in the eyes of foreign partners. Under the current International Sanctions Act, financial institutions are able to control sanction subjects on a "list" basis, but more than list-based checks are needed. In order to identify, mitigate and manage sanction risks, a financial institution must have a good framework of compliance that corresponds to the activities of, and risks inherent in, a financial institution. A good compliance framework will help financial institutions to prevent infringements of sanctions and prevent them from being exploited to circumvent financial sanctions. Consequently, the obligations of persons subject to a special obligation are specified. These are mainly financial institutions whose liabilities are specified. In particular, there will be an obligation to establish an international system of sanction risk mitigation and risk management. By using a risk management system, it will be better able to identify persons subject to financial sanctions and to apply the necessary measures to those persons, for example by freezing assets. A risk-based approach and the application of due diligence measures are not a new obligation for financial institutions. They currently have such obligations under the Money Laundering and Terrorist Financing Prevention Act. This draft specifies that the same principles need to be applied to the control of financial sanctions. Consequently, these obligations are generally familiar to persons with specific obligations, and the establishment or consolidation of a framework for the compliance of financial sanctions does not require a large amount of additional resources or changes. As a significant change, misdemeanours are also included in the draft in order to impose penalties for breaches of the obligations imposed. The misdemeanours imposed help to ensure the correct application of financial sanctions. It is also important to point out that an evaluation of the Council of Europe Committee of Experts on Combating Money Laundering and Terrorist Financing is underway, which also assesses whether Estonia applies financial sanctions in accordance with international standards. If shortcomings in implementation are found, Estonia can get a poor assessment of this in its assessment. This may lead to Estonia being publicly referred to as high-risk countries with shortcomings in the prevention of money laundering and terrorist financing. This means that companies and persons here will be treated in other countries as clients with higher risks and correspondent relationships with Estonian financial institutions may be interrupted. Thus, the problems of complying with international international sanctions may cause international restrictions on the financial sector and difficulties in dealing with financial institutions in other countries. This would severely undermine Estonia's competitiveness as well as its international reputation. We have therefore planned the entry into force of the Act before the aforementioned expert committee visits to Estonia, which will take place in the first half of March this year. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-01-26_U754	Thank you for that question. What are we talking about now? In the law on sanctions in general, we are dealing with those measures which would enable us to influence the international situation. And here we are also talking about, for example, the obligations that come to the Estonian state either through the UN Security Council resolution or through the European Union. In other words, the commitments that one or the other organisation has put in place to contribute to peace in the world or to the protection of human rights. Accordingly, there will be some type of sanctions against countries, persons, organizational entities or groups to influence these individuals or groups, for example, to prevent war or to re-establish peace. To this end, sanctions, which are compulsory for countries, will be imposed. At the moment, when we are talking specifically about financial sanctions, which may, for example, have been imposed in one case or another, we in Estonia now have to apply these financial sanctions on a list basis, i.e. a person or group, such as the President of Belarus, is... on a list basis. They only look by name at whether or not he's in the register. However, the people who are in these registers are, of course, intended to circumvent these sanctions. And in order to prevent this diversion, we want to move on to this risk-based situation monitoring. Perhaps if the person on the list of sanctions has an activity, company or ship, his company, for example, has a ship that is somewhere in the... register, then the transition to the current risk-based system will provide an opportunity to look at the wider picture and sanction all related real estate, ships or associated companies' finances. This new system is much more comprehensive and effective in this respect than the current control system according to the lists.

ParlaMint-EE_2022-01-26_U809	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Honourable members of the Riigikogu, good evening! In today's session, there is a draft law amending the Creative Persons and Creative Unions Act before you, in which we wish to extend in 2022 the reliefs that have already been introduced in the past two years. Originally, the Act on Creative Persons and Creative Unions was amended in April 2020 when the Honourable Riigikogu dealt with the state's additional budget for 2020, in other words, the measures for the spread of the corona virus caused by COVID disease. Within this framework, a number of restrictions related to the granting of creative support were alleviated in order to allow freelance creators who have lost their income to receive support and to ensure access to healthcare for these people as well. Under the conditions of the pandemic, it is extremely important that all people in Estonia, regardless of their chosen careers, are able to contact a doctor or hospital for help. As the pandemic did not show signs of subsidion, there was a logical need to extend mitigation measures. And my predecessor, Anneli Ott, Minister of Culture, presented to the Riigikogu on 5 April last year a draft amendment to the Act on Creative Persons and Creative Unions, which had also been approved by respected MPs. Thank you very much for these earlier decisions. Today, too, we are in a situation where, as a result of a pandemic, some creative people have limited access to work and income. The difficult times are, of course, throughout the cultural sphere, but the greatest risk is those who may not have access to the healthcare system when they need it most. In order to resolve this concern, we ask for Parliament's consent and we wish to extend the reliefs previously approved and introduced this year as well. I would like to remind you of the content of the reliefs that Parliament has previously approved twice. Firstly, the main aspect of the abolition of the mandatory two-year waiting period for the application for creative support. Upon the entry into force of the amendment, a person who lost his or her income could also apply for support if he or she had also done so in 20 and 21. The allowance is granted for a period of six months, with a minimum wage of 654 euros being paid in Estonia. Tax receipts are also guaranteed, which provide access, figuratively speaking, to the services of the Health Insurance Fund, which is an important concern for people in this area. The second important relief concerns the amount of the promised income. In the past, if it was half the minimum wage, it would now be permitted to earn a monthly income of one minimum wage. Thus, the creator will be able to earn a single minimum wage without fear of losing state support during the period when he or she is in a difficult time. Both reliefs would be valid, according to our offer, until the end of this year. As a third relief, which is different from those previously applied, we would introduce an exception for those who have received income in the last three years, either in military service or in parental leave. In 2021, when we had to prove to the Ministry of Culture that he was an active creative person and to provide publicly available works to confirm this, in the case of a person who was not part of the Creative Union, there are now exceptions to this requirement for those who had to focus on other vital responsibilities. As I mentioned earlier, deal with national defence issues or take care of a small child in my activities. It is natural that creative work may not be able to be dealt with during such a special period and should not be an obstacle to obtaining the creative support mentioned in this draft. If the honourable Riigikogu supports our draft, we can implement this package of measures from the spring of this year and until the end of the calendar year. We have the money for this measure in the budget, we are not asking for it further, the amount is slightly more than EUR 1.7 million. ParlaMint-EE_2022-01-26_U860	Thank you, Mr President. Dear Members of the Riigikogu, thank you for this opportunity to present two important drafts to you today at a late hour. The first of these is the draft law amending the Securities Market Act and other laws. This draft submitted to you will change, as the name suggests, particularly the Securities Market Act, but also other financial sector legislation. All amendments relate to the transposition and implementation of EU legislation. Firstly, the draft amends the management and capital requirements of investment firms. Currently investment firms are generally subject to similar management and capital requirements to banks. However, they are not sufficiently flexible and risk-sensitive to investment firms. So now there is a proposal to change the requirements so that they are more proportionate to investment firms. More specifically, according to the draft, investment firms are divided into three different categories on the basis of the size of the assets: large, medium and small. And depending on the category of investment firm that falls into, it will be subject to the corresponding requirements. For the smallest investment firms, the regulatory burden is reduced quite significantly. And it has to be said that investment firms established in Estonia are mostly in the middle category, some of them probably also in the category of small investment firms. Among other things, the initial capital requirements of investment firms will be amended. For example, the initial capital requirement is reduced for investment firms that mediate offers of securities or manage the assets of private clients. Their capital requirement is reduced from EUR 125000 to EUR 75000. In addition, if the investment firm organises an alternative exchange itself, the capital requirement will fall significantly from EUR 730000 to EUR 150000. However, if the investment firm acts as a broker or advises investors, the corresponding capital requirement will slightly increase from 50000 to EUR 75000. In addition, the conditions for the payment of the remuneration of members of the management board of investment firms and other executives and, in particular, the conditions for the payment of performance fees are amended. According to the draft, the payment of performance fees must take more account of the risks associated with a particular manager in an investment firm and of the outcome of his work over a longer period of time, i.e. simplified: no performance fee can be paid by a loss-making investment firm. In addition, this draft provides for additional powers for the Financial Supervision Authority to require investment firms to hold additional capital and liquid funds. This is the case where supervision has shown that risks are higher than normal for the investment firm concerned. The second part of the amendments concerns the simplification of the provision of investment services. These changes are originally driven by the situation caused by the corona crisis. More broadly, the aim is to reduce the regulatory burden on banks and other investment service providers so that they can re-allocation resources and continue to ensure funding for the real economy. This amendment reduces requirements in particular in cases where services are provided to professional investors. Perhaps the obligation to provide information will be reduced, provided that professional investors themselves are able to better analyse and retrieve information about investments. At the same time, when providing investment services to retail investors, the requirements remain essentially as stringent as they have been. The third amendment relates to the increase of the additional ceiling for the reimbursement of deposits by the State guarantee scheme, by the Guarantee Fund. If, at present, the overall compensation rate for each depositor is up to EUR 100000 per bank and, in addition, the Guarantee Fund has to provide additional cash of EUR 70000 if a person has recently sold residential property and this money has recently been received into his account, i.e. the maximum deposit compensation rate is currently EUR 170000. It now increases the ceiling for this additional compensation from EUR 70000 to EUR 100000 and provides that it covers not only real estate sales but also funds received as a result of other social events. For example, if the account receives amounts due in connection with redundancy or also because of an insurance benefit. So in future, the maximum amount of compensation will be EUR 200000, now, as has been said, EUR 170000, hereinafter EUR 200000, EUR 100000 + EUR 100000. Good Riigikogu, I ask you to support this bill. Thank you.
